Q(\mathtt{ch})=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}
Y=\{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}\}
Q(\mathtt{ch})=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}
Y=\{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{3},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}\}
Q(\mathtt{ch})=\epsilon
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{3}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{3}

(b)

The frontier graph
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}
for
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap}\rangle

Figure 4
.
A
VCh
instance (
4(a)
) and its frontier graph (
4(b)
), witnessing the consistency of
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap}\rangle
.
There is a path from source (dotted node) to sink (dashed node),
and the events labelling this path form a valid concretization,
i.e.,
\sigma=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{3}\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}
.
Therefore,

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap}\rangle
is consistent.

Time complexity.

Given Lemma
3.1
, we can solve
VCh
by constructing
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}
and solving standard graph reachability on it.
Recall that each node is a tuple
\langle{Y},{Q},{I}\rangle
.

{Y}
is a
po
-downward closed set, and there are at most

(n^{t}/t^{t})
many distinct subsets of
\mathsf{S}
of this form (using AM-GM inequality).
For each fixed
{Y}
, the number of different possible
{I}
is upper bounded by
t+1
, since
{I}
is either
\bot
or points to the last event of a thread in
{Y}
.
Finally, consider the component
{Q}
.
For any asynchronous channel
\mathtt{ch}
,
the number of messages in
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})
is

\ell=\mathsf{num}_{Y}(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}))-\mathsf{num}_{Y}(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}))
.

A naive counting approach would enumerate the last
\ell
sends to channel
\mathtt{ch}
in every thread,
forming a potential set of unreceived sends of size
t\ell
.
Selecting
\ell
elements from this set yields a total of
O((tk)^{k})
possible combinations, as
\ell\leq k
.
However, our counting method is more refined.
We notice that the sequence of events in the queue
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})

is uniquely determined by the sequence

\tau_{i_{0}},\tau_{i_{1}}\ldots,\tau_{i_{\ell-1}}

of their thread identifiers — if
\tau_{i_{\ell-j}}=\tau
,
then the
j^{\text{th}}

last
event
e
in
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})

belongs to thread
\tau
and, further
e
is the
m^{\text{th}}

last send event on channel
\mathtt{ch}
that thread
\tau
performs, where

m=|\{z\,|\,\tau_{z}=\tau,\ell-j\leq z\}|
.
Since the number of threads is
t
,
the total number of possible sequences corresponding to
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})
is thus

\leq t^{\ell}\in O(t^{k})
.
This implies that the total number of different values that
{Q}
can take on
is in
O(t^{km})
.
Thus, the total number of nodes of
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}
is

O(n^{t}/t^{t}\cdot t\cdot t^{km})
.

We now count the number of edges in
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}
.
Each node has at most
t
out-degree since the set
{Y}
is
po
downward closed for each node.
Hence the number of edges in
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}
is bounded by

(n^{t}/t^{t}\cdot t^{2}\cdot t^{km})
.
Thus, the time for reachability checking is
O(|V|+|E|)=O(n^{t}\cdot t^{km})
.

The graph can be constructed using a simple worklist algorithm.
The worklist is initialized with only the source node.
The algorithm proceeds by repeatedly extracting a node
v
from the worklist and inserting its successors until the worklist is empty.
To compute the successor node
v^{\prime}
of the current node
v
by extending
v
with event
e
,
we first copy
v
into
v^{\prime}
and update
v^{\prime}
according to the rules of the frontier graph.
Copying
v
takes
O(n)
time
and updating
v^{\prime}
takes constant time, and thus time to construct the graph
is
O(n\cdot V+E)=O(n^{t+1}\cdot t^{km})

as in Theorem
1.4
.

The algorithm for
VCh-rf
has similar flavor to that for
VCh
, but relies on a different frontier graph.

Frontier graph for
VCh
-rf
.

The reads-from frontier graph
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}^{{\textsf{rf}}}
of
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is slightly different from
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}
.
First, for a node
v=\langle{Y},{Q},{I}\rangle
,
the set of unmatched send events buffered in
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})
and
{I}
is already determined by
{Y}
and
rf
.
Therefore, we only need to consider the permutations of these events in
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})
.
Moreover, for an edge
v_{1}\xrightarrow{e}v_{2}
labeled with a receive event
e=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch})
over an asynchronous (resp. synchronous) channel
\mathtt{ch}
, we require that the first entry
f=v_{1}.{Q}(\mathtt{ch})
(resp. unique element
f=v_{1}.{I}
) is such that
(e,f)\in{\textsf{rf}}
.
The following lemma states how
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}^{{\textsf{rf}}}
captures the consistency of
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
.

Lemma 3.2.

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent iff there is a sink node reachable from the source node in
G_{\mathsf{frontier}}^{{\textsf{rf}}}
.

Time complexity for
VCh
-rf
.

For each node, the set
{Y}
, together with
rf
, uniquely determine send events that are unmatched, giving us a better bound on the number of possible values for the
{Q}
and
{I}
components of the node.
The number of distinct
{Y}
sets is still
(n^{t}/t^{t})
.
For each
{Y}
,
{I}
is uniquely determined by
{Y}
and
rf
.
Likewise, the set of events in
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})
for an asynchronous channel is the set of unmatched send events in
{Y}
, whose size is bounded by
\mathsf{cap}\mathsf{(\mathtt{ch})}\leq k
.
The total number of permutations for
{Q}(\mathtt{ch})
is thus
\leq\mathsf{cap}\mathsf{(\mathtt{ch})}!\leq k!

and is also
\leq t^{k}
as argued before.
Considering all
m
channels, the number of
{Q}
is bounded by
O((\min(k!,t^{k})^{m})
.
In total, the number of nodes in the graph is

O(n^{t}/t^{t}\cdot(\min(k!,t^{k})^{m})
,
while the number of edges is

O(n^{t}/t^{t}\cdot t\cdot(\min(k!,t^{k})^{m})
,
thereby concluding Theorem
1.5
.

3.2.
VCh-rf
with Synchronous Channels

We now focus on
VCh-rf
in the case where all channels are synchronous and present a linear-time algorithm for Theorem
1.9
.
Previous work shows that purely synchronous communication enjoys some sort of “deterministic replay”, which implies a
O(n\cdot t)
-time consistency algorithm
(
Sulzmann and Stadtmüller, 2018b
)
.
Here we show that this setting admits a linear-time solution, irrespectively of the number of threads.

Our algorithm is based on the following insight.
Since all channels are synchronous, every pair of events
(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv})
related by reads-from must execute consecutively.
Our algorithm packs such event pairs in a single atomic event,
and checks whether all atomic events can be scheduled in a way that respects partial order dependencies due to
po
.
In turn, this reduces to checking for cycles in a suitably defined graph.
We now make the above insight formal.

We assume wlog that the input instance
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{X}=\langle\mathsf{S},{\textsf{po}}\rangle
, is such that each send (resp. receive) event has exactly one receive
(resp. send) event matched to it using
rf
, and the two events belong to different threads.
Otherwise, the instance is clearly inconsistent.

The send-receive graph.

The
send-receive graph
of
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is a directed graph
G_{\mathsf{sync}}=(V,E)
where
V
is the node set and
E
is the edge set, defined as follows.

(1)

V\subseteq\mathsf{S}\times\mathsf{S}
is the set of matching send and receive pairs, i.e.,
\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}\rangle\in V
iff
(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv})\in{\textsf{rf}}

(2)

edges
E
capture
po
dependencies, i.e.,
(\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}\rangle,\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}\rangle)\in E

iff some
e_{1}\in\{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}\}
is the immediate
po
predecessor of some
e_{2}\in\{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}\}
.

See Figure
5
for an illustration.
The send-receive graph precisely captures consistency, as stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3.

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent iff
G_{\mathsf{sync}}
is acyclic.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{3}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{4}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{3}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})

\tau_{1}

\tau_{2}

\tau_{3}

(a)

VCh-rf
instance
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
with synchronous channels.

\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{4},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}\rangle
\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{3},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{3}\rangle
\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}\rangle
\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}\rangle

(b)

The graph
G_{\mathsf{sync}}
.

Figure 5
.
A
VCh-rf
instance
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
(
5(a)
) and the corresponding send-receive graph
G_{\mathsf{sync}}
(
5(b)
).
As
G_{\mathsf{sync}}
is acyclic,

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent.

Algorithm and time complexity.

Following Lemma
3.3
, the
algorithm for checking
VCh-rf
when all channels are synchronous is
straightforward — construct
G_{\mathsf{sync}}
and check for acyclicity.
For each pair
\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd},\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}\rangle
,
there are at most two immediate
po
predecessors,
so the in-degree of each node is at most 2.
Therefore,
G_{\mathsf{sync}}
has
O(n)
nodes and
O(n)
edges and the time to construct the graph is also
O(n)
.
Checking for a cycle in
G_{\mathsf{sync}}
also takes
O(n)
time, which concludes the proof of Theorem
1.9
.

3.3.
Acyclic Communication Topologies

Finally, we turn our attention to acyclic communication topologies and prove that
VCh-rf
can be solved in quadratic time, establishing Theorem
1.7
.
We first formally define the communication topology of an abstract execution.

Communication topologies.

A set of events
\mathsf{S}
induces a communication topology,
represented as an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
where
V
is the set of threads appearing in
\mathsf{S}
,
and we have
(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})\in E
iff
\tau_{i}
and
\tau_{j}
access a common channel, i.e.,
there exist two events
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{S}
such that
\mathsf{th}(e_{1})=\tau_{i}
,
\mathsf{th}(e_{2})=\tau_{j}
, and
\mathsf{ch}(e_{1})=\mathsf{ch}(e_{2})
.
The communication topology induced by an abstract execution
\mathcal{X}=\langle\mathsf{S},{\textsf{po}}\rangle
is the topology induced by its event set
\mathsf{S}
.

Given two threads
\tau_{i}
and
\tau_{j}
, let
{\mathsf{Channels(\mathcal{X})}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}
be the set of channels accessed by both
\tau_{i},\tau_{j}
,
and
{\mathsf{cap}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}
be the restriction of the capacity function
\mathsf{cap}
to the channels in
{\mathsf{Channels(\mathcal{X})}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}
.
We define
{\mathcal{X}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}
and
{{\textsf{rf}}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}
as the abstract execution obtained from
\mathcal{X}
and reads-from relation obtained from
rf
by only keeping events from
\tau_{i},\tau_{j}
that access a channel in
{\mathsf{Channels(\mathcal{X})}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}
.
Our proof of Theorem
1.7
is based on two key insights.
First, we prove that
VCh-rf
on acyclic topologies is
compositional
:
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent iff
\langle{\mathcal{X}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},{\mathsf{cap}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},{{\textsf{rf}}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}\rangle
is consistent, for every
(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})\in E
.
Second, we show that
VCh-rf
over two threads is solvable in quadratic time, by a reduction to 2SAT on formulas of size quadratic in the size of the input.

Comparison with 2SAT encodings for shared-memory setting.

The 2SAT encodings have also been explored in the shared-memory setting
(
Chalupa et al., 2018
)
, but our work introduces several novel aspects.
First, our encoding captures constraints unique to FIFO channel semantics and bounded capacities (including synchronous and capacity-1 channels),
while naturally extending to unbounded channels without extra constraints.
Second, our acyclic-topology result relies on a new compositionality lemma (Lemma
3.4
), showing that any efficient solution for two threads extends compositionally to arbitrary acyclic systems, enabling direct performance gains from improved two-thread algorithms.

Compositionality.

The compositionality lemma is formally stated as follows.

Lemma 3.4.

Let
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
be a
VCh-rf
instance, and
G=(V,E)
the communication topology of
\mathcal{X}
such that
G
is acyclic.
Then
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent iff
\langle{\mathcal{X}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},,{\mathsf{cap}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},{{\textsf{rf}}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}\rangle
is consistent, for every pair of threads
(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})\in E
.

The intuition behind Lemma
3.4
is as follows.
First, clearly for
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
to be consistent, we must have that
\langle{\mathcal{X}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},{\mathsf{cap}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},{{\textsf{rf}}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}\rangle
is consistent for every two threads
\tau_{i},\tau_{j}
.
The other direction is more interesting.
Consider a thread
\tau_{1}
with two neighbors
\tau_{2},\tau_{3}
in the communication topology,
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),(\tau_{1},\tau_{3})\in E
, such that
\langle{\mathcal{X}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}},{\mathsf{cap}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}},{{\textsf{rf}}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}\rangle
and
\langle{\mathcal{X}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{3}},{\mathsf{cap}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{3}},{{\textsf{rf}}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{3}}\rangle
are consistent, witnessed by the corresponding executions
\sigma_{1,2}
and
\sigma_{1,3}
.
Then we can interleave
\sigma_{1,2}
and
\sigma_{1,3}
in any way that respects the program order of thread
\tau_{1}
, and the resulting execution
\sigma_{1}
will be well-formed.
This is because, owning to the acyclicity of
G
, we have
(\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\not\in E
, meaning that
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{3}
do not communicate over a common channel.
In turn, this implies that the interleaving of events from
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{3}
in
\sigma
cannot violate the well-formedness of
\sigma_{1}
.
Composing all executions along edges of
G
in such a way results in an execution
\sigma
that witnesses the consistency of
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
.

The case of
t=2
threads.

Given Lemma
3.4
, we now focus on the case of
VCh-rf
over
2
threads,
when every channel is capacity-unbounded, has capacity
1
, or is synchronous (i.e., the setting captured in Theorem
1.7
).
We obtain a quadratic bound based on two insights.
First, for each channel, channel-related constraints on the order of events accessing it can be encoded as 2SAT.
The search for well-formed execution must also satisfy transitivity constraints, i.e., if
e_{1}\to e_{2}
and
e_{2}\to e_{3}
, then
e_{1}\to e_{3}
.
Transitivity involves three events, and thus does not immediately fit our 2SAT approach.
Our second observation is that, with
2
threads, every three events
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
, must contain two events in the same thread, thus already ordered by
po
.
Then, transitivity can be succinctly captured by a 2SAT formula as well.
In the following we make these insights formal.

Consider a
VCh-rf
instance
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
where
{\mathcal{X}}=\langle\mathsf{S},{\textsf{po}}\rangle
is an abstract execution involving two threads
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
.
We construct a 2SAT formula
\varphi_{\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle}
over propositional variables
x_{e,f}
, where
e,f\in\mathsf{S}
.
Assigning
x_{e,f}=\top
means ordering
e
before
f
in the execution witnessing the consistency of
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
.
Overall,
\varphi_{\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle}
is a conjunction of 8 subformulae:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle}\equiv\varphi_{\textsf{exactly-1}}\land\varphi_{{\textsf{po}}}\land\varphi_{{\textsf{rf}}}\land\varphi_{\textsf{unmatched}}\land\varphi_{\textsf{FIFO}}\land\varphi_{\textsf{trans}}\land\varphi_{\textsf{cap=1}}\land\varphi_{\textsf{sync}}

We now proceed with defining each subformula.

Exactly one.

This formula requires that the order of two events must be resolved exactly in one way.

\displaystyle\varphi_{\textsf{exactly-1}}\equiv\bigwedge\limits_{e,f\in\mathsf{S}}\left(x_{e,f}\implies\neg x_{f,e}\right)

Program order.

This formula requires that the order of two events must respect
po
.

Reads from.

This formula requires that each receive event is ordered after its matched send event.

\displaystyle\varphi_{{\textsf{po}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge\limits_{(e,f)\in{\textsf{po}}}x_{e,f}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{{\textsf{rf}}}\equiv\bigwedge\limits_{(e,f)\in{\textsf{rf}}}x_{e,f}

Unmatched sends.

This formula requires that all unmatched send events are scheduled after all send events that have a matching receive event.
Given a channel
\mathtt{ch}
, let

\displaystyle\textsf{Unmatched}_{\mathtt{ch}}

\displaystyle=\{e\in\mathsf{S}\,|\,\mathsf{op}(e)=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd},\mathsf{ch}(e)=\mathtt{ch},\nexists f\text{ s.t. }(e,f)\in{\textsf{rf}}\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\textsf{Matched}_{\mathtt{ch}}

\displaystyle=\{e\in\mathsf{S}\,|\,\mathsf{op}(e)=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd},\mathsf{ch}(e)=\mathtt{ch},\exists f\text{ s.t. }(e,f)\in{\textsf{rf}}\}

denote the set of unmatched and matched send events, respectively.
We have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\textsf{unmatched}}\equiv\bigwedge\limits_{\scriptsize\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}\mathtt{ch}\in\mathsf{Channels(\mathcal{X})},e\in\textsf{Matched}_{\mathtt{ch}},\\
f\in\textsf{Unmatched}_{\mathtt{ch}}\end{array}\end{aligned}}x_{e,f}

FIFO.

This formula requires that the order of two receive events on the same channel matches the order of the corresponding send events.

\displaystyle\varphi_{\textsf{FIFO}}\equiv\bigwedge\limits_{\scriptsize\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}(e,e^{\prime})\in{\textsf{rf}},(f,f^{\prime})\in{\textsf{rf}}\\
e\neq f,\mathsf{ch}(e)=\mathsf{ch}(f)\\
\end{array}\end{aligned}}\left(\left(x_{e,f}\implies x_{e^{\prime},f^{\prime}}\right)\wedge\left(x_{e^{\prime},f^{\prime}}\implies x_{e,f}\right)\right)

Transitivity.

This formula requires that the ordering of events is transitive.
Let
\mathsf{pred}(e)
(resp.
\mathsf{succ}(e)
) be the unique event (if one exists) that precedes (resp. succeeds)
e
in
po
.
If
\mathsf{pred}(e)
(resp.
\mathsf{succ}(e)
) doesn’t exist,
then
\mathsf{pred}(e)=\bot
(resp.
\mathsf{succ}(e)=\bot
) .
We have
\varphi_{\textsf{trans}}\equiv\varphi^{\mathsf{pred}}_{\textsf{trans}}\land\varphi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{\textsf{trans}}
, where

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lcr}\varphi^{\mathsf{pred}}_{\textsf{trans}}\equiv\bigwedge\limits_{e,f\;\in\;\mathsf{S},\;e^{\prime}=\mathsf{pred}(e)\neq\bot}(x_{e,f}\implies x_{e^{\prime},f})&&\varphi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{\textsf{trans}}\equiv\bigwedge\limits_{e,f\;\in\;\mathsf{S},\;f^{\prime}=\mathsf{succ}(f)\neq\bot}\left(x_{e,f}\implies x_{e,f^{\prime}}\right)\end{array}

Capacity.

This formula requires that the capacity constraints of channels
\mathtt{ch}
with
\mathsf{cap}(\mathtt{ch})\leq 1
are met.
In particular, for two different send events
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})\neq\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
, the matching receive event of the earlier send event also precedes the other send event.
For a synchronous channel, we encode the fact that send and receive events are consecutive.
For asynchronous channels that are capacity-unbounded, we do not need any capacity constraint.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lcr}\varphi_{\textsf{cap=1}}&\equiv&\bigwedge\limits_{\scriptsize\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}\left(e,f\right)\in{\textsf{rf}},e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{S},\mathsf{op}(e)=\mathsf{op}(e^{\prime})=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}\\
\mathsf{ch}(e)=\mathsf{ch}(e^{\prime})\text{ is asynchronous}\end{array}\end{aligned}}\left(x_{e,e^{\prime}}\implies x_{f,e^{\prime}}\right)\\
\varphi_{\textsf{sync}}&\equiv&\bigwedge\limits_{\scriptsize\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}\left(e,f\right)\in{\textsf{rf}},\mathsf{ch}(e)\text{ is synchronous}\\
e^{\prime}=\mathsf{succ}(e),f^{\prime}=\mathsf{pred}(f)\end{array}\end{aligned}}\left(x_{f,e^{\prime}}\land x_{f^{\prime},e}\right)\end{array}

The following lemma states the correctness of the encoding.

Lemma 3.5.

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent iff
\varphi_{\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle}
is satisfiable.

Finally, observe that the number of propositional variables
x_{e,f}
is bounded by
n^{2}
,
while the number of clauses is also
O(n^{2})
.
Since 2SAT is solvable in time that is linear in the size of the formula
(
Aspvall et al., 1979
)
, together with Lemma
3.5
, we arrive at an algorithm that solves
VCh-rf
for
2
threads in
O(n^{2})
time.

Acyclic topologies.

We now have all the ingredients to solve
VCh-rf
on acyclic communication topologies.
Given an input
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
, the algorithm iterates over all edges
(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})
of the communication topology of
\mathcal{X}
, and uses the 2SAT encoding to decide the consistency of
\langle{\mathcal{X}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},{\mathsf{cap}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}},{{\textsf{rf}}}{\downharpoonright}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{j}}\rangle
.

For analyzing the time complexity, observe that every two events
e,f\in\mathsf{S}
appear in some propositional variable
x_{e,f}
of at most one 2SAT instance.
In particular, let
\tau_{1}=\mathsf{th}(e)
and
\tau_{2}=\mathsf{th}(f)
.
If
\tau_{1}\neq\tau_{2}
, then
x_{e,f}
appears in the 2SAT instance of the topology edge
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
.
On the other hand, if
\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}=\tau
, then
x_{e,f}
appears in the 2SAT instance of the topology edge
(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
, where
\tau^{\prime}
is the unique thread accessing the channels that
e
and
f
operate.
We thus arrive at Theorem
1.7
.

4.
The Hardness of Verifying Channel Consistency with Reads From

We now present some of the hardness results for
VCh-rf
.

We first present how channels can be used to encode
atomicity gadgets
– that is,
how to execute a sequence of events from the same thread without interleaving with other threads,
as this encoding will be required in later reductions.

We then show that the problem is intractable for case (i) and (iii) stated in Theorem
1.6
in Section
4.2
and Section
4.3
).
In Section
4.4
,
we prove the quadratic lower bound of
VCh-rf
on
2
threads, as stated in Theorem
1.8
.
The other lower bounds of
VCh
and
VCh-rf
stated in Theorem
1.1
,
Theorem
1.2
,
Theorem
1.3
,
Theorem
1.6
,
are proven with reductions of similar flavor, and appear in Appendix
B

and Appendix
C

due to space limits.

4.1.
Atomicity Gadgets

Our reductions in later sections make use of
atomic blocks
of events as gadgets.
An atomic block
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\sf atomic}
in a thread is a sequence of events such that any two such blocks
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\sf atomic}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\sf atomic}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}2}}
cannot overlap in any concretization.
Here we show how to construct atomicity gadgets, both by using channels with capacity
1
, and by using channels with unbounded capacity.
The latter might sound counter-intuitive, in the sense that send operations to capacity-unbounded channels never block.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}({\ell})
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\sf atomic}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}1}}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\ell)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}({\ell})
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\sf atomic}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}2}}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}({\ell})

\tau_{1}

\tau_{2}

(a)

Atomicity using capacity
1
channel.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}({\ell_{1}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}({\ell_{2}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}({\ell_{2}})
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\sf atomic}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}1}}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}({\ell_{1}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{3}({\ell_{2}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{4}({\ell_{1}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}({\ell_{1}})
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\sf atomic}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}2}}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{3}({\ell_{2}})

\tau_{1}

\tau_{2}

(b)

Atomicity using two capacity-unbounded channels
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}

Figure 6
.
Gadgets for implementing atomic blocks using capacity
1
(
6(a)
) or unbounded-capacity channels (
6(b)
).
Reads-from edges are represented by arrows.

Atomicity with capacity 1.

The atomicity gadget relying on channels of capacity 1 is shown in Figure
6(a)
, using one channel
\ell
,
which resembles a lock.
The thread that sends to
\ell
first fills the channel capacity, and must execute the corresponding receive before the other thread can send to the channel.
The events between the first send and receive are thus executed atomically.

Atomicity with unbounded capacity.

The atomicity gadget using unbounded channels is shown in Figure
6(b)
, relying on two channels
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
.
Its principle of operation is as follows.
If
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\ell_{1})
is executed before
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{4}(\ell_{1})
, then

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\ell_{1})
is also executed before
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{4}(\ell_{1})
, making the atomic section of the first thread execute before the second.
The inverse order is imposed if
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{4}(\ell_{1})
is executed before
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\ell_{1})
,
as this orders
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{3}(\ell_{2})
before
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\ell_{2})
, and the argument repeats.

We note that the atomicity gadgets can be generalized to an arbitrary number of threads.
For brevity, Figure
6
illustrates the case for two threads only.

4.2.
Hardness with Asynchronous Channels of Capacity
1

We establish a reduction from the
VSC-read
problem
(
Gibbons and Korach, 1997
)
.
An instance of the
VSC-read
problem is a tuple
{\mathcal{X}}=\langle\mathsf{S},{\textsf{po}},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
, where
\mathsf{S}
is a set of events of the form
\langle{\tau,\mathsf{r}(x)}\rangle

or
\langle{\tau,\mathsf{w}(x)}\rangle
,
in which
\tau
is a thread identifier and
x
is
a memory location,
po
is the per-thread total order (a.k.a program order)
and
rf
maps each read event to a write event of the same register.
Such an instance is sequentially consistent (SC) if there is a total order
over
\mathsf{S}
that respects
po
and
rf
, and ensures that
for every
(e,f)\in{\textsf{rf}}
pair on register
x
, there is no other
\mathsf{w}(x)

event ordered between
e
and
f
.

Overview.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\langle\mathsf{S},{\textsf{po}},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
be an instance of
VSC-read
.
We construct an instance
\langle\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{cap}^{\prime},{\textsf{rf}}^{\prime}\rangle
of
VCh-rf
,
where
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\langle\mathsf{S}^{\prime},{\textsf{po}}^{\prime}\rangle
.
At a high level, each write event (and each read event) in
\mathcal{X}

is mapped to a sequence of send and receive instructions in
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
that essentially appear atomically in every concretization.
Further the reads-from relation of
\mathcal{X}
is also accurately
reflected in
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
through reads-from on channels.

Reduction.

Figure
7
illustrates the reduction on a small example.
The set of threads in
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}

is the same as
{\mathcal{X}}
.
The set of channels used in
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}

is
\{\mathtt{ch}^{i}_{x}\,|\,x\in\mathcal{R},1\leq i\leq m_{x}\}\uplus\{\ell\}
,
where
\mathcal{R}
is the set of registers accessed in
{\mathcal{X}}
,

m_{x}=\max\{p_{e}\,|\,e\text{ is a write on }x\}

and
p_{e}
is the number of read events
f
with
(e,f)\in{\textsf{rf}}
.
The capacity function
\mathsf{cap}
assigns capacity
1
to every channel.
At a high level, the thread-wise event sequences in
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}

are structurally similar to those in
{\mathcal{X}}
,
and can be characterized using a map
M
that maps
events in
\mathsf{S}
to distinct atomic, thread-local sequences of events in
\mathsf{S}^{\prime}
,
so that
\mathsf{S}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{e\in\mathsf{S}}\{f\,|\,f\in M(e)\}
.
Atomicity is guaranteed by channel
\ell
with capacity 1.
In Section
4.1
, we have detailed an explanation about atomicity gadgets.
We now describe the map
M
.

\mathsf{w}_{1}(x)
\mathsf{r}_{3}(y)
\mathsf{r}_{1}(x)
\mathsf{r}_{2}(x)
\mathsf{w}_{2}(y)

\tau_{1}

\tau_{2}

(a)

A
VSC-read
instance.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathsf{w}_{1}(x)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}_{y}^{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathsf{r}_{3}(y)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathsf{r}_{1}(x)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathsf{r}_{2}(x)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{y}^{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell})
\mathsf{w}_{2}(y)

\tau_{1}

\tau_{2}

(b)

The corresponding
VCh-rf
instance.

Figure 7
.

A
VSC-read
instance (
7(a)
) and the corresponding
VCh-rf
instance (
7(b)
) with channel capacities of
1
.

For a write event
e=\langle{t,\mathsf{w}(x)}\rangle
,
M(e)
is a sequence
of
m_{x}
-many
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}
events, followed by
m_{x}-p_{x}

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}
events,
all enclosed in a block of send-receive pair on channel
\ell
;
the thread identifier of each of the following event is
\tau
,
and we omit explicitly mentioning it.

\displaystyle M(e)=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathsf{\ell})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{1})\cdots\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{m_{x}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{p_{e}+1})\cdots\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{m_{x}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathsf{\ell})

Let us now discuss the encoding of read events.
For this, we assume some arbitrary ordering
\{f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{p_{e}}\}

of the set of read events reading from some write event
e
.
Then, the event sequence corresponding to the
i^{\text{th}}

read event
e=\langle{\tau,\mathsf{r}(x)}\rangle
of some write event is:

\displaystyle M(e)=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathsf{\ell})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{i})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathsf{\ell})

The program order
{\textsf{po}}^{\prime}
is then obtained by considering all pairs
of events of the form
(e_{1},e_{2})
in
\mathsf{S}^{\prime}
such that
either they belong to
M(e)
for some
e
and
e_{1}
appears before
e_{2}
in
M(e)
,
or they belong to
M(e)
and
M(e^{\prime})
respectively with
(e,e^{\prime})\in{\textsf{po}}
.
The
{\textsf{rf}}^{\prime}
relation is also straightforward.
For each event of the form
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\ell)
in
M(e)
,
its corresponding send event is the unique
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\ell)
event in
M(e)
.
The send and receive events on channels of the form
\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{i}
are paired
as follows.
Let
(e,f_{i})\in{\textsf{rf}}
be a pair of write and its
i^{\text{th}}

read event in
\mathsf{S}
.
Then the send event
e^{\prime}_{i}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{i})
in
M(e)
is paired to
the event
f^{\prime}_{i}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{i}))
in
M(f_{i})
(i.e.,
(e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i})\in{\textsf{rf}}^{\prime}
).
Further, the unmatched send event

e^{\prime}_{j}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{j})
in
M(e)
, where
p_{e}+1\leq j\leq m_{x}

is paired with the
(j-p_{e})^{\text{th}}
receive event
f^{\prime}_{j}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}_{x}^{j})
in
M(e)
,
(i.e.,
(e^{\prime}_{j},f^{\prime}_{j})\in{\textsf{rf}}^{\prime}
).

The correctness of the construction is relatively straightforward, and stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1.

{\mathcal{X}}
is SC consistent iff

\langle\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{cap}^{\prime},{\textsf{rf}}^{\prime}\rangle
is consistent.

We now argue about the time taken to construct
\langle\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{cap}^{\prime},{\textsf{rf}}^{\prime}\rangle
.
Each write event in
\mathsf{S}
can be observed by at most
|\mathsf{S}|
different read events.
Each
e\in\mathsf{S}
is thus mapped to a sequence consisting of
O(|\mathsf{S}|)
events.
Thus,
|\mathsf{S}^{\prime}|\in O(|\mathsf{S}^{2}|)
, which concludes case (i) of Theorem
1.6
.

4.3.
Hardness with
3
Threads,
5
Channels and no Capacity Restrictions

We show that
VCh-rf
remains intractable when both the number of threads and channels are constant,
and there are no restrictions on channel capacities.

Events in Phase-

Figure 8
.

Reduction from 3SAT to
VCh-rf
with capacity-unbounded channels.
Events with double boundary do not appear in Phase-
0
.
Events marked with
\star
only appear when the
q^{\text{th}}
literal in clause
C_{j}
is over variable
x_{i}

Overview.

Starting from a 3SAT instance
\psi
with

{n_{c}}
clauses
C_{1},\ldots,C_{{n_{c}}}
over
{n_{v}}
propositional variables

\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{{n_{v}}}\}
,
we construct a
VCh-rf
instance
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle

with
3
threads
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3}
and
5 channels
\mathtt{ch}_{1},\mathtt{ch}_{2},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
.
Informally,
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
consists of

{n_{c}}+1
phases, arranged sequentially.
The first
initialization
phase (‘Phase-0’) picks an assignment of boolean
values for each variable.
The remaining
{n_{c}}
phases encode the requirement
that at least one literal from each clause is set to true.
Phase-
j
(with
j\geq 1
) duplicates the assignment to all variables
from the previous phase and checks if the chosen
assignment makes clause
C_{j}
true.
Figure
8
depicts this scheme.

Reduction.

The sequence
\sigma_{r}
corresponding to events of thread
\tau_{r}
(
r\in\{1,2,3\}
)
is of the form
\sigma_{r}=I^{r}\cdot A^{r}_{1}\cdot A^{r}_{2}\cdots A^{r}_{{n_{c}}}
.
The sequence corresponding to Phase-
0

is of the form
I^{r}=I^{r}_{1}\cdots I^{r}_{{n_{v}}}
,
where
I^{r}_{p}
picks an assignment to variable
x_{p}
in thread
\tau_{r}
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccc}I^{1}_{p}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})&&I^{2}_{p}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})&&I^{3}_{p}=\epsilon\end{array}

Next, the sequence corresponding to thread
\tau_{r}
and Phase-
j
(
j\geq 1
)
is of the form
A^{r}_{j}=A^{r}_{j,1}\cdots A^{r}_{j,{n_{v}}}\cdot B^{r}_{j}
.
where
A^{r}_{j,p}
corresponds to variable
x_{p}

and
B^{r}_{j}
encodes the satisfaction of clause
C_{j}
(see Figure
8
for illustration).
We describe these components next.

A^{3}_{j,p}=\epsilon
for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,{n_{c}}\}
,
p\in\{1,\ldots,{n_{v}}\}
.
When
r\in\{1,2\}
, then
A^{r}_{j,p}
is used to encode the variable
x_{p}
in clause
C_{j}
of thread
\tau_{r}
.
If
x_{p}
appears in clause
C_{j}
and it is the
p^{\text{th}}
literal of
C_{j}
(p
\in\{1,2,3\}
),
then:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}A^{1}_{j,p}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{\bot}(c_{q})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\\
A^{2}_{j,p}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{\top}(c_{q})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\end{array}

If
x_{p}
is not in clause
C_{j}
, then:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}A^{1}_{j,p}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\\
A^{2}_{j,p}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\end{array}

Finally,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccc}B^{1}_{j}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\top}(c_{1})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\bot}(c_{2})&&B^{2}_{j}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\top}(c_{2})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\bot}(c_{3})&&B^{3}_{j}=\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\top}(c_{3})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\bot}(c_{1})\end{array}

We now discuss the reads-from mappings.

•

The receive events
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
,
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
,
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
and
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
in
A^{1}_{j,p},A^{1}_{j,p},A^{2}_{j,p},A^{2}_{j,p}
are respectively mapped
to the send events
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
,
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\bot}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
,
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
,
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}^{p}_{\top}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
in
A^{1}_{j-1,p},A^{1}_{j-1,p},A^{2}_{j-1,p},A^{2}_{j-1,p}
(or in
I^{1}_{p},I^{1}_{p},I^{2}_{p},I^{2}_{p}
if
j=1
).

•

Let
C_{j}=\gamma_{1}\lor\gamma_{2}\lor\gamma_{3}
such that
\gamma_{q}
is either
x_{j_{q}}
or
\neg x_{j_{q}}
.
For each
q\in\{1,2,3\}
, we have the following. If
\gamma_{q}=x_{j_{q}}
, then we require that the receive event
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\top}(c_{q})
reads from send
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{\top}(c_{q})
in
A^{2}_{j,j_{q}}
, and
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\bot}(c_{q})
reads from
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{\top}(c_{q})
in
A^{1}_{j,j_{q}}
.
Otherwise, we require that
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\top}(c_{q})
reads from
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{\bot}(c_{q})
in
A^{1}_{j,j_{q}}

and
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{\top}(c_{q})
reads from
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{\top}(c_{q})
in
A^{2}_{j,j_{q}}
.

The following lemma states the correctness of the above construction.

Lemma 4.2.

\psi
is satisfiable iff

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent.

Finally, the number of events in
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is
O({n_{v}}+{n_{c}})
,
which concludes case (iii) of Theorem
1.6
.

4.4.
Quadratic Hardness with
2
Threads

Finally, in this section we prove the quadratic hardness of
VCh-rf
over just
2
threads when either all channels have capacity
1
or have no capacity restrictions.
We achieve this by establishing a fine-grained reduction from the Orthogonal Vectors problem (OV)
(
Williams, 2005
)
.

The Orthogonal Vectors problem.

The OV problem takes as input two sets
A=\{a_{1},a_{2}\ldots,a_{n}\},B=\{b_{1},b_{2}\ldots,b_{n}\}\subseteq 2^{\{0,1\}^{d}}
,
each containing
n
boolean vectors in
d
dimensions.
The task is to determine whether there are two vectors
a\in A,b\in B
such that
a
and
b
are orthgonal, i.e.,
\langle a\cdot b\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}a[i]\cdot b[i]=0
.
Under the SETH, OV cannot be solved in time
O(n^{2-\epsilon})
, for every fixed
\epsilon>0
, as long as
d=\omega(\log n)

(
Williams, 2005
)
.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{1}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)
\vdots
\vdots
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{n}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{n}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{n}}(\alpha)
A_{\mathsf{init}}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{n}}(\alpha)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{n}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{n}})
\vdots
\vdots
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{1}})
B_{\mathsf{init}}
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\gamma)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\beta)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{1}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{n}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{n}})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\delta)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{B}(\beta)
A_{1}
B_{n}

\tau_{A}

\tau_{B}

(a)

Events for
A_{\mathsf{init}},B_{\mathsf{init}},A_{1},B_{n}

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{2}}(\alpha)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\beta)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{2}}(\beta)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{2}})

A_{n}

\tau_{A}
cont.

\tau_{B}
cont.

(b)

Events for
A_{i},B_{n-i}
(
i\geq 2
)

Figure 9
.
General scheme of the reduction from Orthogonal Vectors to
VCh-rf
with unbounded channels under two threads.
Send/receive events on the same channel and with the same subscript are related by
rf
.

Overview.

We construct a
VCh-rf
instance
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
which is consistent iff
A
and
B
contain an orthogonal vector pair.

\mathcal{X}
comprises two threads
\tau_{A}
and
\tau_{B}
, respectively containing events encoding the vectors of
A
and
B
.
Figure
9
illustrates the overall scheme.
At a high level, the reads-from edge due to the pair
\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\gamma),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\gamma)\rangle\in{\textsf{rf}}
triggers an orthogonality check between the vectors
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
.
The reduction is built in such a way that this process of inference, called
saturation
(formally described in Section
5.1
), simulates orthogonality comparisons of the vectors.
If
a_{1}[i]=b_{1}[i]=1
for some
i
, witnessing that
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
are not orthogonal, the corresponding events encoding
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
will contain two sends on the same channel,
which triggers an orthogonality check between
a_{1}
and
b_{2}
.
If
a_{1}
and
b_{2}
are also not orthogonal,
then
a_{1}
and
b_{3}
are compared, and so on.
If the check between
a_{1}
and
b_{n}
fails, this triggers the check between
a_{2}
and
b_{1}
, and the process continues, until an orthogonal pair is found, or the check between
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
does not identify an orthogonal pair.
The fact that
a_{n},b_{n}
are not orthogonal implies
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\delta)
must be ordered before
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\delta)
, which contradicts with
\langle\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\delta),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\delta)\rangle\in{\textsf{rf}}
,
implying that the constructed instance is not consistent.

We now formally describe the reduction.
For simplicity, for certain events
e
, we use subscripts to indicate the specific vector that
e
represents.

Reduction for capacity-unbounded channels.

Given the OV instance
A,B
, we construct the corresponding
VCh-rf
instance using two threads
\tau_{A}
and
\tau_{B}
and channels
\{\mathtt{ch}_{1},\mathtt{ch}_{2},\ldots,\mathtt{ch}_{d},\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\}
, all having unbounded capacity.
We describe the events next, while using subscripts in the event operations that ensure that the combination of the operation, the subscript and the channel uniquely identify each event.
Send and receive events on the same channel that also have the same subscript are implicitly related by
rf
.
The events of threads
\tau_{A}
and
\tau_{B}
are organized as follows:

\displaystyle\tau_{A}=A_{\sf init}\cdot A_{1}\cdot A_{2}\cdots A_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{B}=B_{\sf init}\cdot B_{n}\cdot B_{n-1}\cdots B_{1}

Observe that the order of appearance of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
is the reverse of that of
B_{n},\ldots,B_{1}
.
We next describe the contents of each block.
We use the notation
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{i}})
to denote the sequence
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{j_{1}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{j_{2}})\cdots\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{j_{k}})
, where
j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{k}
is the unique increasing sequence of indices in
\{1,2,\ldots,d\}
corresponding to non-zero entries in the vector
a_{i}
.
Likewise,
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{i}})
,
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{i}})
and
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{i}})
expand in a similar fashion.
The init block in
\tau_{A}
contains send events for each vector
a\in A
(on all those channels
\mathtt{ch}_{i}
such that
a[i]=1
) with alternating send events on channel
\alpha
, and likewise in
\tau_{B}
(but in reverse order):

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}A_{\sf init}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{1}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)\cdots\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{n}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{n}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{n}}(\alpha)\\
B_{\sf init}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{n}}(\alpha)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{n}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{n}})\cdots\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{1}})\end{array}

The extremal blocks (
A_{1},B_{1},A_{n},B_{n}
) and internal blocks (
A_{i},B_{i}
where
2\leq i\leq n-1
) are as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}A_{1}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\gamma)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\beta)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{1}})\\
A_{n}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{n}}(\alpha)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{n-1}}(\beta)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\delta)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{n}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{n}})\\
B_{n}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{n}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{n}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\delta)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{B}(\beta)\\
B_{1}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{1}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{B}(\beta)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\gamma)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)\\
A_{i}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{i}}(\alpha)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{i-1}}(\beta)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{i}}(\beta)\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{i}})\\
B_{i}&=&\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{i}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{i}})\cdot\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{i+1}}(\alpha)\end{array}

\displaystyle A:\ a_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\

\displaystyle a_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle B:\ b_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}\

\displaystyle b_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}

(a)

Two sets of vectors
A
and
B
.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
A_{\sf init}

\tau_{A}

(b)

Events for
A_{\sf init}
and
B_{\sf init}
.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\gamma)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\beta)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\beta)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\delta)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{B}(\beta)

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)

(c)

Events for
A_{1}
,

,

, and
B_{2}
.

Figure 10
.
An example for the reduction from the Orthogonal Vectors problem to
VCh-rf
with unbounded channels under two threads. Only cross-thread reads-from edges are shown.

The following lemma states the correctness of the construction.

Lemma 4.3.

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent iff
A
and
B
contain an orthogonal vector pair.

Regarding the time complexity, the reduction takes time proportional to
|A|+|B|
i.e.,
O(n\cdot d)
.
Hence, a subquadratic algorithm for deciding the consistency of
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
would imply a subquadratic algorithm for solving OV, thereby contradicting SETH.
We thus arrive at case (i) of Theorem
1.8
.

Example 0.

Figure
10
illustrates an example with
n=d=2
.
The OV instance consists of the two sets
A=\{a_{1}=\langle 0,1\rangle,a_{2}=\langle 1,0\rangle\}
and
B=\{b_{1}=\langle 0,1\rangle,b_{2}=\langle 1,1\rangle\}
.
Since
a_{2}
and
b_{1}
are orthogonal, the constructed instance

\Gamma=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
is consistent.

We now explain how
\Gamma
encodes orthogonality checks between the
vectors of
A
and
B
via saturation.
Let us use
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
to denote the saturated order of
\Gamma
;
its formal definition is presented in Section
5.1
, though it can be intuitively understood
to be a set of orderings between events of
\Gamma
that must hold
in every consistent concretization of
\Gamma
, if one exists (Lemma
5.1
).
Since
({\textsf{po}}\cup{\textsf{rf}})^{+}\subseteq<_{\mathsf{sat}}
, we have
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)
, which means
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)
.
This signifies that
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
are compared for orthogonality. Since
a_{1}
and
b_{2}
both have value 1 in dimension 2, they are not orthogonal.
This is witnessed by
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
, further leading to
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})
.
Due to
po
, we also have
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)
,
and therefore
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)
, which means that
a_{1}
and
b_{2}
are now compared for orthogonality.
As before,
a_{1}
and
b_{2}
are not orthogonal, so we get the following sequence of inferences:

1.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})\implies\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})

2.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{1}}(\beta)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{B}(\beta)\implies\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\beta)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{B}(\beta)

3.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{2}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{1}}(\beta)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b}(\beta)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)\implies\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{2}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{1}}(\alpha)

Notice that we now start to check orthogonality between
a_{2}
and
b_{1}
.

As
a_{2}
and
b_{1}
are orthogonal, the sequences
\mathtt{ch}_{a_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{a_{2}})
and
\mathtt{ch}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{b_{1}})
do not share any channels. Therefore, saturation stops inferring orderings at this point. However, the receive on
\delta
also implies orderings via saturation.
In fact, this also leads to orthogonality comparisons, but in a reversed order:
b_{2}
is compared with
a_{2}
, then
b_{1}
with
a_{2}
, and so on.
The following sequence of inferences illustrates this:

1.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\delta)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\delta)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})\implies\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})

2.

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{b_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{2}}(\mathtt{ch}_{1})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{a_{2}}(\alpha)\implies\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{2}}(\alpha)

The ordering of
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)
before
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{a_{2}}(\alpha)
is then what compares
b_{1}
to
a_{2}
(since
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{1}}(\mathtt{ch}_{2})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{b_{2}}(\alpha)
). Again, the orthogonality of
a_{1}
and
b_{2}
stops the saturation process.

We claim that the resulting
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
contains no cycle and is strong enough to fully sequentialize
\mathcal{X}
.

Channels with capacity
1
.

Finally, we argue about the quadratic hardness of
VCh-rf
when every channel has capacity
1
.
This result follows a recent result that verifying sequential consistency with a reads-from mapping (
VSC-read
problem) is OV-hard for
2
threads
(
Mathur et al., 2020
)
.
In Section
4.2
,
we have shown for any
VSC-read
instance with
n
events,
we can construct an equivalent
VCh-rf
instance with
O(m_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot n)
events,
where
m_{\mathcal{R}}
is the maximal number of read events that observe the same write event.
Fortunately, in the reduction developed in
(
Mathur et al., 2020
)
,

m_{\mathcal{R}}
is a constant,
and therefore our
VCh-rf
instance is of linear size as the input
VSC-read
problem.
Since
VSC-read
under two threads is OV-hard,

VCh-rf
cannot be solved in
O(n^{2-\epsilon})
time for any
\epsilon>0
,
when there are 2 threads and every channel has capacity 1.
Item (ii) of Theorem
1.8
is thus proven.

5.
Evaluation

We have implemented our frontier graph algorithm for
VCh-rf

and evaluated its performance on 103
VCh-rf
instances
and compare against a constraint-solving approach for
VCh-rf
.
Before we discuss our experimental setup (Section
5.2
) and our evaluation results (Section
5.3
and Section
5.4
),
we describe a crucial optimization,
saturation
in Section
5.1
.

5.1.
Saturation

Saturation, widely used as a pre-processing step in consistency checking
(
Zennou et al., 2020
)

and dynamic race detection
(
Pavlogiannis, 2020
)
, for shared register based concurrency, can be adapted to channel based concurrency.
At a high level, saturation infers additional event orderings in polynomial time before the core consistency-checking algorithm executes.
For a
VCh-rf
instance, saturation infers orderings beyond
the input program order and reads-from relations.
For example, if two send events on the same channel are ordered by
po
,
then their corresponding receive events must also be ordered.

Formally, let
\Gamma=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle

be an input
VCh-rf
instance,
where
\mathcal{X}=\langle\mathsf{S},{\textsf{po}}\rangle
.
The saturated order
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
of
\Gamma

is defined to be the smallest transitive relation on events such that

({\textsf{po}}\cup{\textsf{rf}})^{+}\subseteq<_{\mathsf{sat}}
and:

1.

For each channel
\mathtt{ch}
, and pairs

(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}))\in{\textsf{rf}}
,
(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}(\mathtt{ch}))\in{\textsf{rf}}
, we have

\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
iff
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
.

2.

For each channel
\mathtt{ch}
, if
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})
is a matched send event and
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
is an unmatched send event, then
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
.

3.

For each synchronous channel
\mathtt{ch}
, pair
(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch}))\in{\textsf{rf}}
,
and event
e\in\mathsf{S}
, we have

e<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch})
iff
e<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch})
and
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}(\mathtt{ch})<_{\mathsf{sat}}e
iff
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}(\mathtt{ch})<_{\mathsf{sat}}e
.

4.

For each channel
\mathtt{ch}
with
\mathsf{cap}\mathsf{(\mathtt{ch})}=1
, pair
(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}))\in{\textsf{rf}}
, and event
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})\neq\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})
, we have
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
iff
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch})<_{\mathsf{sat}}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
.

The saturated order
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
preserves the consistency of
\mathcal{X}
, as formalized in
Lemma
5.1
.

Lemma 5.1.

Let
\Gamma=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathsf{cap},{\textsf{rf}}\rangle
be a
VCh-rf
instance and let

<_{\mathsf{sat}}
be the saturated order of
\Gamma
.
If
\Gamma
is consistent, then
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
is a partial order (i.e., does not contain cycles),
and further, each concretization
\sigma
of
\Gamma
(if any) must respect
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
.

Constructing the saturated order.

We remark that
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
can be constructed in at most
O(n^{3})

using a straightforward fix point algorithm.
Our actual implementation is optimized and crucially makes use of
the recently proposed data structure
Collective Sparse Segment Trees (CSSTs)
(
Tunç et al., 2024b
)

designed for saturation-like fix point computations.

Using the saturated partial order.

Based on Lemma
5.1
, saturation enhances
the decision procedure for
VCh-rf
in two key ways.
First, if the constructed order
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
is cyclic, then
we return NO directly, i.e., without explicitly running any further
consistency checking (such as our frontier graph-based or a constraint solving based) procedure.
Second, when
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
is acyclic, then it can be used for
a more aggressive on-the-fly pruning
of the frontier graph.
More specifically, in our frontier graph algorithm,
when exploring outgoing edges of a node
u=\langle Y,Q,I\rangle
,
we can prune all edges
u\xrightarrow{e}v

for which
e
is not enabled in
u
according to the saturated order,
i.e., when
\exists e^{\prime}
, s.t.
(e^{\prime},e)\in<_{\mathsf{sat}}

but
e^{\prime}
is not in the set
Y
of events executed so far.
This optimization significantly reduces the number of paths the algorithm must explore.
We remark that we also augment the constraints (corresponding to the
VCh-rf
problem)
with additional constraints induced by
<_{\mathsf{sat}}
.
However, as we observed in our evaluation, solver-based algorithms cannot benefit
from this acceleration, as saturation increases the number of such constraints,
slowing down performance.

Discussion.

A saturation-style algorithm for consistency checking in
Message Sequence Charts
(MSCs) is proposed in
(
Di Giusto et al., 2023
)
.
Our setting generalizes this approach to a more expressive model.
In MSCs, channels are assumed to be unbounded, and thus no saturation rules are needed to capture capacity constraints.
In contrast, our model supports both bounded and unbounded channels, requiring valid interleavings to respect the capacity limits.
This is reflected in our saturation rules – specifically, rules (3) and (4) correspond to capacities 0 and 1.
While additional rules could model higher capacities, we omit them to preserve the efficiency of the heuristic saturation procedure.

5.2.
Experimental Setup

Given that the problem of consistency checking arises in practical program
analyses, the goal of our evaluation is to gauge how
our algorithms perform in practice and how they compare
against generic solutions such as the use of
\mathsf{SMT}
solvers.

Compared methods and implementations.

For our evaluation we focus on the
VCh-rf
problem and skip
evaluation for
VCh
for two reasons —
VCh-rf
is more prominent
in applications such as model checking and predictive analyses, and
further, in our experimental setup, accurate and fast logging of
values in executions is challenging.
Recall that
VCh-rf
is an
NP
-complete problem in general
and can alternatively be solved using a constraint solving approach such as
the use of
\mathsf{SMT}
solvers, and we also implement such a solution for our evaluation.
We compare the performance between two approaches:
(1) the frontier graph algorithm
\mathsf{FG}
and (2)
\mathsf{SMT}
-based solvers
\mathsf{SMT}
, along with their respective saturated variants (
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
and
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
).
In our
\mathsf{SMT}
encoding, for each event
e
, we use an integer variable

0\leq x_{e}\leq n-1
, representing the position of
e
in a potential concretization.
For each channel
\mathtt{ch}
, we introduce
2n+2

auxiliary integer variables to model (1) the cumulative count of send events and (2) the cumulative count of receive events across all prefixes in a potential concretization
(complete encoding details can be found in Appendix
D.1
).
Recall that the saturation-augmented versions
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
and
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}

pre-emptively reject if saturation produces a cycle.
If saturation succeeds, then in
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
,
we augment the
\mathsf{SMT}
formula with the constraint
x_{e}<x_{e^{\prime}}
,
where
(e,e^{\prime})\in<_{\mathsf{sat}}
and
e^{\prime}
is the earliest event in
\mathsf{th}(e^{\prime})

with this property.
All algorithms are implemented in Java 23 and
\mathsf{SMT}
/
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
use
the Java bindings of
Z3
-4.12.2
(
de Moura and Bjørner, 2008
)
.

Benchmark programs.

Our evaluation subjects comprise two distinct groups.
The first group is primarily derived from GoBench
(
Yuan et al., 2021
)
, a widely used Golang concurrency bug benchmark suite.
GoBench includes 82 real-world bugs from 9 popular open-source projects (GoReal) and 103 bug kernels (GoKer)
(
Saioc et al., 2025
;
Jiang et al., 2023
)
.
From GoReal, we selected 6 projects for evaluation;
the remaining 3 projects were excluded either because we could not log execution traces
from them or the generated logs were too short.
Similarly, we omitted GoKer benchmarks because they produce executions with too few channel operations to be meaningful for our analysis.
The second group consists of additional prominent Golang open-source projects, namely

rpcx
,
raft
,
go-dsp
,
bigcache
,
telegraf
,
ccache
, and
v2ray
, selected to further validate our approach.

Generation of positive
VCh
-rf
instances.

For each benchmark, we first select 1–3 test cases and generate a
totally ordered log of channel events in their runtime execution,
by modifying
ThreadSanitizer

(
Serebryany and Iskhodzhanov, 2009
)
.
We performed a linear time sanity check that each
recorded execution satisfies channel capacity constraints.
Each execution log can now be translated to a
VCh-rf
instance by discarding
the total order between events and only retaining program order and the inferred reads-from relations.
As a result, the instances thus obtained are positive instances,
since the original execution serves as a valid concretization of them.
To evaluate algorithmic scalability,
we additionally generate new
VCh-rf
instances by
keeping varying length prefixes of existing instances
corresponding to long executions (containing thousands to millions of channel accesses).
Detailed statistics of these instances can be found in Appendix
D.2
.

Generation of mutated instances.

Recall from the previous paragraph that
VCh-rf
instances
obtained from real executions are bound to be positive.
To obtain negative instances,
we mutate the previously obtained positive instances by performing targeted modifications
to their reads-from relation as follows.
We randomly select a reads-from pair

(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}))
and another send event
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
on the same channel.
If
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
has a matching receive event
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
,
then we swap the two reads-from mappings, i.e. enforce

(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})),(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}))\in{\textsf{rf}}
.
Otherwise, when
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch})
is not received,
we enforce
(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{2}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}))\in{\textsf{rf}}

and delete the reads-from pair
(\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}snd}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}),\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}rcv}_{1}(\mathtt{ch}))
.
For each positive
VCh-rf
instance with
n
events,
we mutate it
\max(5,0.05n)
times.
While these mutations do not theoretically guarantee inconsistency,
our experimental results show that 88.3% (91/103) of mutated instances
become inconsistent, 8.7% (9/103) remain consistent, while
the consistency status of the remaining 2.9% (3/103) instances could not
be determined due to algorithm timeouts.

Machine configuration and metrics reported.

The experiments are conducted on a 2.0GHz 64-bit Linux machine.
We set the heap size of JVM to be 100GB and timeout to be 3 hours.
For each
VCh-rf
instance, we report key parameters, such as number of events,
threads, channels and maximal channel capacity,
as well as the running time of each algorithm.
Times reported denote average running time over 3 repeated runs.

.

.

Comparison between
\mathsf{FG}
and
\mathsf{SMT}
(Figure
11(a)
).

\mathsf{SMT}
times out on 35 instances due to excessive memory consumption;

\mathsf{FG}
solves all of these successfully.

\mathsf{FG}
fails on only 2 instances that
\mathsf{SMT}
completes.
In addition, when both algorithms succeed,
\mathsf{FG}
outperforms
\mathsf{SMT}

in running time by a factor of 5–50,000
\times

(full statistics can be found in Appendix
D.2
).
These results demonstrate that
\mathsf{FG}
scales significantly better than
\mathsf{SMT}
on most benchmarks.

Comparison between
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
and
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
(Figure
11(b)
).

\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
times out on 90.3% (93/103) instances due to increased formula size,
which leads to higher memory consumption compared to standard
\mathsf{SMT}
.
In contrast,
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
successfully completes 93.2% (96/103) of instances
and can often scale to instances with 50k events.
These results demonstrate that
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}

achieves significantly better scalability than
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
on consistent benchmarks.

Impact of saturation.

Saturation substantially enhances the performance of
our frontier graph algorithm —
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
solves 54 more instances than
\mathsf{FG}
.
Saturation induces a slight slowdown on some instances
(due to the overhead of the fixpoint computation), but this is
largely limited to smaller instances where

\mathsf{FG}
already finishes very quickly.
The impact of saturation on
\mathsf{SMT}
solvers is limited.

\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
successfully solves only 1 additional instance compared to
\mathsf{SMT}
,
and demonstrates significant speed improvements on just 3 benchmarks.
We hypothesize that this marginal gain occurs because saturation increases the
\mathsf{SMT}
formula size.

.

Comparison between
\mathsf{FG}
and
\mathsf{SMT}
(Figure
12(a)
).

\mathsf{FG}
successfully solves 26 more instances than
\mathsf{SMT}
.
For instances where both algorithms complete,
\mathsf{FG}
achieves a speedup ranging from 3
\times
to 3000
\times

(full statistics can be found in Appendix
D.2
).
Compared to its performance on consistent instances,

\mathsf{FG}
solves
4
fewer cases.
This happens for inconsistent
VCh-rf
instances where
\mathsf{FG}{}
performs a complete
traversal of the frontier graph, resulting in increased computational time.

Comparison between
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
and
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
(Figure
12(b)
).

Both algorithms demonstrate strong performance,
successfully completing most instances, with
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
solving 91.3% (94/103)
instances and
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
solving 97.1% (100/103).
The superior performance can be attributed to saturation’s ability to efficiently reject nearly all inconsistent instances before initiating the core consistency checking algorithm.
Notably, among the 6 instances where
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
times out but
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
succeeds,
all are consistent instances (recall that mutated instances are not guaranteed to be inconsistent).
In these cases, saturation not only fails to benefit
\mathsf{SMT}
solvers
but actually degrades their performance due to the increased formula size.

Cumulative number of finished instances over time for each algorithm.

Survival analysis.

In Figure
13
, we present the cumulative number of finished instances over time for each algorithm.
All algorithms reach saturation after approximately 100 seconds,
illustrating the NP-hard nature of the
VCh-rf
problem – additional time does not lead to solving more instances.
For consistent instances,
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
initially completes fewer instances than
\mathsf{FG}
due to the overhead introduced by the saturation process.
However, as time progresses,
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
overtakes
\mathsf{FG}
by efficiently pruning infeasible execution paths.
For mutated instances,
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
behaves almost identically to
\mathsf{SMT\text{-}Sat}
, benefiting from the saturation phase, which filters out inconsistent instances before the main solving procedure begins.
Overall, the survival analysis demonstrates that
\mathsf{FG\text{-}Sat}
consistently solves the largest number of instances within the same time budget.

In summary, the frontier graph algorithm demonstrates
better performance over
\mathsf{SMT}
solving based approach, both with or without saturation.
Frontier graph algorithm successfully completes more instances across all benchmarks.
When both approaches terminate,
the frontier graph algorithm achieves significant speedups ranging from 3
\times
to 50,000
\times
.
Further, we observe that
saturation can significantly enhance the effectiveness of consistency checking in practice.

6.
Other Related Work

Verifying linearizability.

Verifying channel consistency resembles verifying linearizability (
VL
)
(
Herlihy and Wing, 1990
;
Gibbons and Korach, 1997
;
Bouajjani et al., 2018
;
Bouajjani et al., 2017
;
Emmi and Enea, 2019
;
Abdulla et al., 2025
;
Gibbons et al., 2002
;
Lee and Mathur, 2025
)
, which checks whether a concurrent history over a (queue) object is equivalent to some sequential one.
Unlike
VCh
,
VL
enjoys locality, enabling a linear-time decomposition into per-object histories.
Moreover,
VL
typically operates on interval partial orders, making it simpler than
VCh
.
Finally,
VL
over queues reduces to
VCh
in linear time.

Message sequence charts.

A closely related problem is that of Message Sequence Charts (MSCs)
(
Madhusudan, 2001
;
Alur et al., 2005
;
Di Giusto et al., 2023
;
Genest and Muscholl, 2005
)
, where threads communicate via peer-to-peer channels. Among these, the work of Di Giusto et al.
(
Di Giusto et al., 2023
)
is most relevant. However, our problem strictly generalizes MSCs:
(i) send and receive events in
VCh
need not be paired a priori;
(ii) both
VCh
and
VCh-rf
allow channels shared by more than two threads;
(iii) the same thread pair may communicate over multiple channels; and
(iv) channels may have bounded capacities.

Our communication model, inspired by languages like Go, assumes channel-based FIFO semantics where all threads can access any channel, with operations on each channel totally ordered.
Consequently, their results do not directly extend to our setting.
Indeed, in
(
Di Giusto et al., 2023
)
, it is shown that the consistency checking problem for these communication models can be solved in polynomial time, because the consistency predicate is MSO definable.
In contrast, the consistency checking problem for our setting is NP-hard.

Model checking.

The
SPIN
model checker
(
Holzmann, 1997
)
provides support for programs with message passing, and the
GOMELA

(
Dilley and Lange, 2022
)
bounded model checker extends it to support Go programs and uses
SPIN
as its backend.
Given an LTL formula
\psi
and a program
P
,
SPIN
constructs an automaton for
\neg\psi
and
P
,
and then searches for traces accepted by their product.
If such a trace exists, the property
\psi
does not hold for
P
.
That is,
SPIN
does not explicitly address the consistency-checking problem.
Moreover,
\mathsf{MUST}

(
Enea et al., 2024
)
is another model checker that operates under the message-passing semantics of MSCs.
While
\mathsf{MUST}
implicitly performs consistency checks as part of its model checking algorithm,

(
Enea et al., 2024
)
does not investigate the complexity-theoretic aspects of these checks.

Register consistency checking.

The consistency checking problem for registers has been extensively studied in prior work
(
Gibbons and Korach, 1997
;
Gibbons and Korach, 1994
;
Cantin et al., 2005
;
Chen et al., 2012
)
.
As demonstrated in this paper, channel consistency checking is strictly harder than register consistency checking due to a key difference in their semantics: registers can only retain the most recent write event, whereas channels can remember up to capacity send events.
Related algorithms have also been developed for consistency checking under weak memory models, including TSO
(
Manovit and Hangal, 2006
;
Hu et al., 2011
;
Chini and Saivasan, 2020
)
and C11
(
Tunç et al., 2023a
;
Chakraborty et al., 2024
)
.

Predictive analysis.

Predictive analysis is a dynamic analysis technique that takes a program execution as input and reorders it to expose potential concurrency bugs.
Recent work has developed predictive algorithms for detecting data races
(
Mathur et al., 2021
;
Pavlogiannis, 2020
;
Flanagan and Freund, 2009
;
Ang et al., 2025
;
Shi et al., 2024
;
Mathur et al., 2018
;
Farzan and Mathur, 2024
;
Kulkarni et al., 2021
)
, deadlocks
(
Kalhauge and Palsberg, 2018b
;
Tunç et al., 2023b
)
, atomicity violations
(
Flanagan et al., 2008
;
Mathur and Viswanathan, 2020
)
and more general properties
(
Ang and Mathur, 2024b
;
Ang and Mathur, 2024a
)
.
These algorithms typically compute a candidate set of events and attempt to serialize them into a witness execution, often using a consistency checking oracle.
Thus, predictive analysis can be viewed as a downstream application of consistency checking.
However, existing prediction algorithms almost exclusively target shared-memory concurrency, neglecting executions involving message-passing via channels.
Our work bridges this gap by establishing theoretical foundations for channel-based predictive analysis.

7.
Conclusion

Consistency testing is a fundamental task for analyses of concurrent programs such as model checking and predictive testing.
We conducted a thorough complexity-theoretic investigation for
this problem for the message-passing programming paradigm with
FIFO
channels.
We have developed novel algorithms and established hardness results for a range of inputs parameters.
We further implemented and empirically evaluated the performance of our algorithms.
Together, our upper and lower bounds reveal an intricate complexity landscape and our evaluation
of our algorithms demonstrate their effectiveness in practice.
Future work includes applying these algorithms to partial order reduction based model checking and predictive testing for message-passing languages such as Go.
